**The rule that closes it has to account for flooring.** A filesystem stamps a modification time by truncating the current time to its own granularity. So a write that lands *after* a read can record a time *before* it. "Older than the read" therefore proves nothing, because the granule can still be open. Measured on NTFS, that mistake serves a stale diff on about one same-length rewrite in four hundred, and on a 1s-granule volume it would go wrong on most of them. So a fingerprint is trusted only once a **full granule** passes between the stamp and the read. The margin is a constant that must be an upper bound on real granularity: **2 seconds**, since FAT and exFAT quantise to 2s and HFS+ to 1s, where NTFS, ext4 and APFS are milliseconds or finer. That trades redundant diffs of files written in the last two seconds, which are files that just changed, for never showing a stale one. **It is a table lookup rather than a measurement because measuring the right filesystem would need a probe write.** §11.1 carries "the monitor writes nothing" as a rule rather than as an accident of what this code happens to do. The whole case for the margin rests on that rule, so the two have to be one decision.

**What it still cannot see** is a writer that restores a modification time it did not advance: `cp -p`, `rsync -t`, `unzip`, `touch -r`. Git carries the inode change time to catch those. `std` exposes no equivalent on Windows, so closing the gap is a dependency decision rather than an implementation detail. It is tracked on the deferral shelf and not assumed away.

**And it assumes the local clock stamps the modification time.** The margin compares a filesystem timestamp against the clock of this process. On a network filesystem the skew of the server subtracts directly from the margin: a server two seconds behind consumes the whole margin, and the flooring problem returns. Local worktrees are unaffected, and a monitor beside an agent watches local worktrees.

## 7. Testing


- **Snapshot tests over `ratatui::backend::TestBackend` with `insta`**: render frames into an in-memory buffer, snapshot as text. This is what makes I5 and I6 assertable at all: the UI becomes diffable text.

- **`criterion`** for I4, I7 and I9, **tracking rather than gating.** Criterion compares a run against a saved baseline, and the budgets in §3 are absolute. So it is the right instrument for "this got 20% slower" and the wrong one for a pass/fail line. It is compiled in CI so the benchmarks cannot rot. It is not timed there, because a shared runner cannot produce a number worth comparing.

- **Budget gates** in `crates/vigia-core/tests/budgets.rs`, in **two tiers**, because an absolute wall-clock threshold is a strong instrument on a known machine and a weak one on a hosted runner:

  - *Structural* gates compare the engine against itself across fixtures that differ only in how much changed. They are ratios and exact byte counts, so they are hardware-independent, take **no slack**, and are what actually catches a regression. Making the frame path re-read every changed file is a 5x wall-clock change that a generous threshold lets through, and a 100x byte-count change that these cannot miss.

  - *Absolute* gates hold the wall clock to §3. They run in release only, since the budgets were set against optimised code. They use a slack multiplier (`VIGIA_BUDGET_SLACK`, default 1) so that the variance of a shared runner does not read as a code regression.


**A breached p99 is re-measured once, and only a second breach fails.** *Ruled 2026-08-17, session* ([#178](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/178)), after a third gate failed with its median comfortably inside budget and its tail an order of magnitude out. Wall clock includes time the process was not running. So on a two-vCPU hosted runner one descheduling quantum puts two samples of two hundred and fifty outside the budget, which **is** the ninety-ninth percentile. Two rounds fix that without weakening anything, because interference is independent between rounds while a regression recurs by construction. **What is deliberately not done**: raising the slack, which hides a regression exactly as well as a stall, and trimming the percentile, which weakens the tail claim the budget *is*.


**And what a second breach means is decided by thread CPU time rather than by the shape of the distribution** ([#212](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/212)). A median inside budget with a tail outside it is *consistent with* a stall and does not establish one. CPU time establishes it, because no amount of host contention inflates work done. The comparison is the **off-CPU time of a round against the time that round spent above budget** (`Samples::excess_over`). If the time the process spent not running covers the total excess of the round, the host is a sufficient explanation, so the breach is reported and not failed. **Both sides are sums over the round** ([#269](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/269)). If a round is compared against the overshoot of the p99 itself instead, ordinary per-frame scheduling noise out-accumulates a per-frame overshoot, and the test drifts toward acquittal as the sample count rises. **Reporting a covered breach rather than failing it is the only weakening in this ruling.** It uses totals rather than a CPU percentile, because `GetThreadTimes` is quantized to the scheduler tick and a 3ms frame reports zero CPU on Windows. Where a platform has no thread clock, an unattributable breach is **ours**, which is the only safe direction. **What CPU time cannot separate is preemption from our own blocking**: a frame that started waiting on the disk reads here as a busy host, and the structural tier covers that. The gate that proves the clock sees work at all runs for eight of those ticks (125ms of 15.625ms each) and passes at half, so a host that takes a tick or two from it cannot read as a blind clock.


- **A budget measured on one target is a budget about that target, and until 2026-08-08 the shipped Linux binary was not the one measured.** Every absolute gate ran on `ubuntu-latest`, which is glibc, while §9 makes `x86_64-unknown-linux-musl` the **only** Linux artifact a release produces. So the artifact a Linux reader receives had never been held to I7 or I9 at all. The `musl` job in `ci.yml` now runs the absolute gates against that target. This costs no runner change, because a static musl test binary executes natively on a glibc host. [#12](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/12).


**It runs `-p vigia`, and the reason is a constraint worth knowing.** The unscoped form does not compile for a cross target. `criterion` is a dev-dependency of `vigia-core`, it pulls `alloca`, and the build script of `alloca` is `cc`. Then `cc` looks for `x86_64-linux-musl-gcc`, which a hosted runner does not have. **So the pure-Rust property `CLAUDE.md` protects holds for the shipped graph and does not hold for the dev graph on a cross target.** It is recorded here rather than hidden by installing a C cross-compiler in CI.


**The concern that motivated it was unfounded.** On `ubuntu-latest`, release, musl, `VIGIA_BUDGET_SLACK=3`: **first paint 14.49ms** against I7's 50ms. A bulk rewrite of 100 undrawn files inside the settle margin gave **p50 11.96ms, p99 12.46ms** over 270 in-margin frames against I9's 16ms. Every gate is green, and first paint holds even unslacked. The numbers are in the log because the step passes `--nocapture`. Libtest discards the output of a passing test, so a run without it shows that the budgets hold and records nothing about by how much.


**The absolute gate in `first_paint.rs` is release-only through `absolute_gates_apply`, and until 2026-08-08 no CI job selected it in release, so it ran nowhere.** Both the `test` and `musl` jobs now run it **in a step of its own**. Cargo runs test binaries one at a time but chooses the order. Sharing one invocation put the three cold starts of `first_paint` over a 100 x 500 fixture *between* the two `budgets` binaries. So the absolute gates of the shell began measuring immediately after a burst of exactly the disk and allocator work they are most sensitive to ([#36](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/36)). That is the recurring shape of this section, reached through the test **selector**: the gate was correct, warm, positioned and non-vacuous, and nothing ran it.


- **An invariant the engine can only make possible gets a second gate over the caller**, in `crates/vigia/tests/`. I4 is the case. The core fetches content per file, so painting the top of a large diff without reading the bottom is *available*. Nothing in the core stops a renderer from asking for every file anyway, and asking is the natural way to write one. **Either tier can be the one that has to move there**, and I2b needed both. `reads.rs` holds the structural half and `crates/vigia/tests/budgets.rs` holds an *absolute* one, because highlighting follows the viewport and so happens in the shell. A frame timed in the core is a frame with the syntax parser missing.


**Seven rules below are one family: a gate can be correct and still measure the cheapest corner of the space it is about.** Each was found by a real defect passing a green suite, and each names a different corner.


- **A budget measured at one position is measured at its cheapest one.** A caller starts at the top, so a gate that never scrolls tests the corner where the least work is possible. The frame gate of I2b ran at row zero and passed while a frame four screenfuls down cost 53ms against a 16ms budget, sustained and with no input. Its own assertion was right and was unable to fire. Any gate over a windowed view is parameterised by where the window is.

- **A gate that warms up cannot see a first-paint cost, and a first-paint budget therefore needs a gate that never warms.** A steady-state gate is *right* to discard its warmup. So the discarding is a blind spot to cover elsewhere, not a flaw to fix. Every steady-state wall-clock gate drops fifty frames, and I7 is a budget about exactly one frame, yet its only gate was in `vigia-core`, where that gate builds no `Highlighter`. The shipped first paint was **105.03ms** against a 50ms budget, 2.1x over, for two phases. It was invisible because two correct decisions met. **A budget over the first N of anything needs a gate whose window starts at zero.** [#51](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/51), `crates/vigia/tests/first_paint.rs`.

- **A gate for a *deferred* cost asserts the work was deferred, not merely that the clock is small.** The fix for a slow first paint is to do less on it, so the subject of the gate can quietly become *drawing less*. `first_paint.rs` asserts that the body is full and that the frame parsed zero lines **before** it asserts the clock. It also asserts that a later frame parses more than zero. Without that, a build whose highlighter never runs satisfies the whole gate. It also compares the rows of the plain frame against those of the coloured one. It does this at the `Row` level, which is one level short of the cell a reader sees, and [#106](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/106) is that gap named rather than left implied. [#129](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/129) needed the same pairing for a second deferral. `warm.rs` asserts that a grammar that nothing has compiled parses **zero** lines and draws every span plain. It also asserts, in the same file, that the same hunk colours once the warm lands. That second assertion is in the same file because a gate for an absence alone is satisfied by a build that never highlights anything.

- **A frame-time gate that never paints times half a frame.** It went unnoticed longest because the omission is invisible from inside the gate. Every budget gate in this repo timed `Frame::advance` plus `App::view` and stopped. So `render`, where the *width* of a row is decided, was outside both tiers on both crates. A row carrying **7.2x more line than pane** passed a 16ms gate for two phases, and what found it was a reader scrolling a Japanese README. **A gate is written against the whole frame of the caller, and any stage left outside it is a stage nothing can regress you on.** `crates/vigia/tests/paint.rs`, [#45](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/45).

- **A structural gate bounds a counter, not a resource, so a cost added outside the population of that counter is invisible to it.** This is the line above one axis over. There a *stage* was left outside the timer. Here every stage was inside and the gate was still blind, because the new syscall landed in `Worktree` while `FrameStats::probes` counts the frame path. [#15](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/15) needed the type of a file before reading it, and the obvious `lstat` put a **second `stat` per file** on exactly the path that `reads.rs::a_tick_inside_the_settle_margin_stats_each_file_once` exists to hold at one. It stayed green. The clock was unable to cover for it either, because the gate for that fixture deliberately refuses to assert a wall clock.


**And removing the cost was not the end of it, which is the half worth keeping.** The syscall was made merely *conditional* and was still counted nowhere. Deleting the fix and restoring the unconditional `lstat` then left **every test in the repository green**. An optimisation whose whole justification is a syscall count had no failing test, in the one repository whose method is *"an invariant without a failing test is a wish"*. The probe is counted across the layer now. `Worktree::diff_counted` threads a `&mut u64` that `FrameStats::probes` accumulates, so a `stat` taken anywhere under it lands in the frame's own count. The gate fails with its own unchanged message, **"the tick took 200 stat calls over 100 files"**. **Completing the population of a counter is not the same act as widening a gate to admit a regression.** One lets a regression through, and the other catches one that was previously invisible. **When a change adds work near a counter, ask which population the counter has, not whether its gate is green.** Ask again after the fix, because a saving the instrument cannot see is a saving the next reader can delete.


- **A fixture over ASCII cannot tell a column bound from a character bound**, because over ASCII they are the same number. The budget fixtures are generated source, 34 columns a line, so *"a row costs the pane"* and *"a row costs the whole line"* produced identical counts across every gate in the suite. Anything measured in **rendered width** needs a fixture where width and length disagree: `Scratch::wide_lines_as` is 531 columns over 412 characters of Japanese, emoji and Latin.

- **A fixture population sitting on the default configuration of its tooling cannot observe the code that exists for the non-default.** This is the same family one axis further out. There the fixtures were uniform in their *content*. Here they were uniform in how they were *built*. `Scratch` creates every fixture. It shells out to `git init` and sets `core.autocrlf false`, so both sides of every diff were always LF. The code path that skipped the clean filter produced byte-identical output to the one that runs it. Not one test was wrong and not one had a way to fail. [#65](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/65) drew a file that `git diff` called unchanged as a 1,790-line rewrite while the suite was green, and what found it was pointing the tool at a real repository. **The tell is checkable: delete a normalisation, filter or conversion step and see whether anything goes red.** That is a mutation test with a specific target: "does any fixture make this branch's presence observable", rather than "is this branch covered". The answer is to **span** the axis rather than move along it, so `Scratch::crlf_worktree` stands beside `Scratch::new` instead of replacing it. Git alone offers `autocrlf`, `.gitattributes` filters, `core.symlinks`, `core.ignorecase` and sparse checkout. A fixture built by `mkdir` and `write` has silently taken the same position on all of them.


**`core.symlinks` was the next one along and it was holding two defects, which is the strongest evidence this rule has produced.** It was named above as an unspanned axis before anyone had looked. [#15](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/15) spanned it and found a symlink diffed as the contents of its *target*, where git stores the target **path**. That defect was known and filed. The second was not. `frame::fingerprint` used `fs::metadata`, and **Windows refuses to resolve a symlink whose stored target uses forward slashes**, which is exactly what git stores. So the fingerprint was `None` on every checked-out link, and every symlink in a Windows worktree was re-diffed on **every frame, indefinitely**, against I2a. No gate would have caught it, because the axis had no fixture on it. `crates/vigia-core/tests/frame.rs::a_repointed_symlink_is_not_reused_from_the_targets_fingerprint` is where to restore the following `stat`, and `settle` then reports *"still re-reading after 8 idle frames"*. **An axis named as unspanned is a prediction, and this is the one that paid.**


- **A gate whose setup materialises every unit measures the cheapest cache population.** This one hid longest, because the helper responsible is *named* for the other thing it does. `settle()` exists to wait out the staleness margin, and it proves the wait worked with `materialise()`, which diffs **every** file. A frame holding a `FileDiff` for every changed file rebuilds every height from memory and reads nothing. So the walk that the narrowing of I4 admits cost exactly zero in every gate that opened with `settle`, on both tiers, in both crates. The bug it hid was not small. The height walk re-read the whole undrawn changed set on **every tick**: 94 files and 3.7 MiB over the hundred-file fixture, 18.36ms p99 against 16ms, with eleven read-bounding gates green over it. **If the fixture setup of a gate populates the cache the gate is about, the gate measures the case where that cache is full.** `settle_spans` is the same wait without the materialisation and stands beside `settle` rather than replacing it, which is the same span-the-axis answer as `crlf_worktree`. [#101](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/101).

- **A gate that settles before it measures measures the cheapest state.** This is the same mistake along a different axis: time rather than position. `settle()` exists so that a fixture written moments ago can be *proved* unchanged, and every structural gate in `reads.rs` calls it first. So the settle margin, the one window in which the frame path deliberately recomputes instead of reusing, was the one window nothing measured. The claim in §10 that the margin breaches I9 under a bulk rewrite went two phases with no gate on either tier, in either direction. **An invariant that governs a window needs a gate that runs *inside* it.** [#32](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/32).

- **A gate can assert the defect it was named against, and the tell is an exact small count where the rule is a bound.** `crates/vigia/tests/scroll.rs::the_bottom_of_the_diff_is_content_rather_than_blank` was written against a blank pane, said so in its name, said so again in its comment, and then asserted `view.rows.len() == 1` against a twenty-two row body. Every word around it was right, and the one line that runs pinned the failure in place. That is worse than an absent gate, because nobody goes looking for a defect that has a green gate over it. [#57](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/57) was found by a reader watching an agent run `git reset --hard`, two phases after the gate shipped. **The signature is a specific small number where the rule is about a bound.** "The screen is full" is `== height`, never `== 1`. It is legible by reading the assertion against the name of the test.


- **An invariant whose two failure modes are not symmetrical gets a gate for each.** I2a is the case. Reusing too *little* is slow and loud, and the budget gate catches it. Reusing too *much* is fast, passes every budget, and shows a diff that no longer exists. So `crates/vigia-core/tests/frame.rs` compares every reused frame against one computed with no memory at all. With a budget gate alone, the second failure would pass as a success.

- **A rule covering a window too small to race is extracted as a pure function and tested directly.** A filesystem cannot reach the racily-clean guard in §6 on demand. Dropping it leaves the whole integration suite green, and only a unit test over the decision function goes red. Where a correctness rule has no reachable integration path, the pure-function test *is* the gate.

- **Gates are mutation tested before they are trusted.** Break the code deliberately, make sure that the gate goes red, and restore. Two of the flaws found this way were invisible to reading: a structural gate comparing one call against the sum of its own calls, and a p99 over too few cold samples.

- **Steady-state budgets are sampled after a warmup**, and over enough frames for a percentile to be one. I9 is a claim about steady state, so the cold path is outside its scope by definition. Measured cold frames run ~40ms against a warm p99 of ~3ms, and at 30 samples a nearest-rank p99 is just the maximum.

- **A CI guard fails the build if `cc`, `cmake` or `bindgen` enters the dependency graph** on any tier-1 target, plus a musl build asserting the binary links no shared libraries. The pure-Rust constraint is what makes musl-static and Windows cheap, so it is enforced rather than trusted.

- **`proptest`** over diff parsing and hunk-boundary logic.


### The soak, and the instrument that gates nothing


- **The soak drives the whole pipeline, not the engine.** I3 is a claim about the process a reader leaves open, so measuring `vigia-core` alone would prove it only about a program nobody runs. The harness is `vigia::run` with the terminal removed. It has a real `notify` watch thread, real coalescing, `Frame::advance` per tick, follow and scroll through `App`, `View::collect` driving the `Highlighter`, and `render` into a `ratatui` buffer. What it leaves out is named rather than discovered later. It leaves out the terminal takeover, which needs a tty and is I8's. It leaves out the input thread. It leaves out the signal handler, which is process-global. It leaves out index writes, because the loop reaches the same mass-eviction state by reverting files, and keeping `git` out of the measured window is what makes the temp-file gate exact.

- **The soak is two tiers, like every other budget here.** The *structural* gates are counters and directory entries, so they run in every `cargo test`. They are: the retained caches bounded by the current diff and by the viewport, eviction actually happening, and **zero temp files retained**. The *absolute* gate is RSS drift, and it is the one that has to be scheduled.

- **A drift gate over a window shorter than its own warmup is measuring warmup.** RSS climbs to an allocator plateau in the first minutes of any process and only then goes flat. A short window reads that climb as a leak, and a generous threshold would then let a real leak through. The gate discards a warmup prefix and compares the median of the first quarter of what remains against the median of the last quarter. It **refuses to assert at all** below a window where that leaves two ends worth comparing. Refusing is the point: a gate that cannot say "no drift" has not been tested, and one that says it from four samples is worse than absent.

- **The warmup prefix of the gate is workload-dependent, the report says so, and that is a ruling rather than an omission.** The prefix is a **fraction** of the window, 10%, which is window-independent and is what it was chosen for. It is not *workload*-independent. RSS plateaus in about **thirty seconds** over the default 20 x 200 fixture and in about **eight hours** over 100 x 500. A tenth of a 24-hour run therefore discards 2.4 hours of a climb that takes eight. The first full window read **13.26% against the 5% budget** on a process that moved 0.33 MiB across the sixteen hours past its plateau. That is a **known instrument artifact**, and it is the case that §10 anticipated as "crosses on variation rather than on a leak".


**The answer is a measured number printed beside the verdict, not a moved baseline.** The report carries where the series settled and what the same ends-ratio says from there: `settled at sample 97 of 288 (8.08h in), drift from there 1.85%` beside the 13.26% of the gate. The settled **level** is the median of the last quarter. A rolling window of that same quarter width steps back from the end while its median stays within **1%** of the level. Nothing is gated on it.


**A measured baseline was built three times and does not ship.** Each formulation fell to a real adversarial shape, and each defect lived inside the previous fix. A leak between two plateaus was absorbed as warmup (**4.24%** against a 20.00% window). One 4 KiB page prepended to a flat head defeated the clause added to stop that (**4.77%** against 29.50%). A warmup followed by a flat stretch followed by a leak passed through the same clause again (**4.99%** against 25.00%). The shared root is that a walk finds the *end* of a plateau, while a gate would need its whole *shape*. That is change-point detection, which is a discipline rather than a patch. It is out of proportion here, because two things already discharge the leak-catching duty: the calibrated-leak fixture, which is caught from the fraction unchanged, and the structural tier. **The gate keeps its one honest property, which is that it can be wrong only in the loud direction.** A red that the printed plateau explains is an annotation. A green produced by an unsound detector is a laundering, and a suite stays green while that happens. `crates/vigia/tests/soak.rs::statistic` holds every clause, with the recorded 288-sample series included as a fixture. The three detector designs and the shapes that defeated each are on [#127](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/pull/127).


- **The same report carries the shape the gate rounds off, and the gradient through it, and neither is gated.** A ratio between two ends cannot tell a process that stepped once and went flat from one that is climbing, and that difference is the whole of the open question in §10 about I3. So the report prints the medians of a quarter's worth of samples at **four** positions and a least-squares gradient in **MiB per hour**. The gradient is fitted against elapsed time rather than sample index, so it survives a run whose loop fell behind. The gradient is **signed** where the ratio of the gate is deliberately clamped. A gate must not pass for the wrong reason, and a diagnostic whose job is a sign must not hide one. The middle pair and the gradient are reported and never gated. A threshold on either would add a budget this document does not name.


**A gradient is printed with the span it was fitted over**, because for one fixed amount of RSS wander the reported MiB/h goes as the reciprocal of the window. Reference machine, by window: **+364.71 MiB/h** at 15s, **+7.89** then **+2.74** at 600s, **+1.94** and **−0.70** at 900s, **+0.92** at 3600s. A span is about nine tenths of its window at 288 samples and **three quarters** at the fifteen-second default, where `MIN_SAMPLES` rather than the warmup fraction sets it. **Without the span beside it none is comparable to another**, which is the comparison this section asks a reader to make. The instrument becomes readable somewhere around the window that the budget of I3 names. `soak.rs::statistic::the_gated_ends_are_the_first_and_last_quarter_medians` holds the outer two in place, over two fixtures because each hides what the other catches.


- **A bound is only evidence when something reached it, so a gate over one asserts that eviction happened as well as that the bound held.** I10 is the case. The per-commit soak window touches about eighty distinct paths against a cap of 256 and never turns the 120-second window over once. So `tracked <= 256` is satisfied there the way an empty room satisfies a fire code. The soak **refuses to assert** when the run reached neither eviction rule, and it prints why, exactly as the drift gate does. The deterministic proof is a fixture that drives ten thousand paths in every `cargo test`. Verified by running the soak at 300 files, where it reports 256 tracked at the cap with 207 evicted.


- **Every instrument above measures a fixture, so one of them measures a worktree instead, and it gates nothing.** `crates/vigia/tests/observe.rs` is `vigia::run` with the terminal taken out and with **no writer of its own**. It is pointed at an existing checkout, and the agent in the other pane is the workload ([#72](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/72)). It asserts nothing because its window is whatever a working session happened to be, so a threshold over either would add a budget this document does not name. Three things about its shape are the rules of this section turned on itself. **The oracle runs in a separate process.** That is because answering "does this diff agree with `git`" diffs every changed file, which is the setup-materialises-every-unit trap aimed at the very caches whose cost is the measurement. **A budget is a bucket edge.** "Was a frame ever over 16ms" has to be a *count*, so 16000µs is a declared boundary and the accessor **refuses** any threshold that is not one. **Nothing unbounded is retained**, because the process holding the samples is the process whose RSS is one of the readings. So the worst eight frames are kept with their context, and everything else is a bucket.


**And a harness that claims to be the whole frame of the caller has to be it, which this one was not on its first attempt.** `drive` left three stages out, and each understated a real cost. `branch_for` reads `.git/HEAD` on exactly the frames that draw the empty state, which over the recorded sessions was about half of them. The `chrome` is built **twice** per paint. And `Shell::draw` settles the repaint debt of I7 **inside the same tick**, so the first frame a reader sees is painted twice. A harness that painted once timed the plain half and displaced the colour onto the frame after: a first frame of **5.52ms** became **558.83ms**, which is where the grammar compile actually lands. That last one is the mechanism this section already records the product nearly losing, lost again in a second hand-copy of the same sequence. That is the argument for a gate rather than for a reading. `observe.rs::wiring` drives one `draw` over a real worktree and asserts that the debt is settled and the frame is coloured. It runs over **two** fixtures, because a dirty tree never reaches the branch read at all.

## 8. Phases

Live status, issue-linked, is in [`ROADMAP.md`](ROADMAP.md). This section is the shape. That file is the state. Work is taken one task at a time via `.claude/skills/take-next/`.

**Phase 1 — core engine.** `vigia-core` plus a `main` that prints frame timings. Prove `gix` gives working-tree-vs-index diffs at the fidelity and speed needed. It is the least-precedented dependency in the stack and everything sits on it. Land **I1, I2a, I4, I7, I9**, each gated. No TUI.

**Phase 2 — minimum monitor.** ratatui + crossterm shell. Follow mode (I5), scroll, mouse, exit safety (I8), 40-column layout (I6). Plus **I2b** (needs `syntect`) and **I3** (soak). Snapshot suite.

**Phase 3 — glanceability.** Section 5, plus theming.

**Phase 4 — the artifacts tell the truth.** The public surfaces (README, mockup, spec, tracker) agree with the code and with each other.

**Phase 6 — measured, not assumed.** Claims that outran their evidence get their measurement: the workload the thesis is about, the window I3's budget names, the default-view ruling a week of use decides. **Closed 2026-08-07, with the two rows that need a *duration* moved to the Shelf rather than finished**. The workload's own window accrues one session at a time, and the default-view ruling waits on it. A milestone that cannot close until somebody has used the tool for a week is a calendar rather than a queue. The measurement each row was blocking has been taken. What neither row has is repetition.

**Phase 7 — distribution.** `cargo-dist`, crates.io publish, personal Homebrew tap, prebuilt binaries on GitHub releases.

**Phase 8 — look and feel.** Layout, colour, keys and chrome. It comes after distribution on purpose: the minimal real thing ships first, and polish lands as post-release momentum guided by the real-use evidence rather than ahead of it. **Missing from this list until 2026-08-09**, when [#12](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/12) corrected the README's phase table and found the same gap here. That is the drift the parenthetical below already records, one phase later: this section is the shape and `ROADMAP.md` is the authority on numbering, so a phase that exists there and not here means this file is behind again.

**The Shelf** (milestone `Shelf`, titled "Phase 5" until 2026-08-06): work found mid-phase and consciously shelved, taken only by deliberate choice, never "next". It holds no place in the sequence at all. That is why the phase number was retired: a number claims a place.

*(This list said "Phase 4 — distribution, Phase 5 — deferred items" until 2026-08-05, a whole phase-numbering behind the roadmap it defers to. The pre-flight cannot see this file's own phase prose, because it compares invariant tokens and milestone titles. So the drift sat here across two renumberings. This section is the shape. `ROADMAP.md` is the state and the authority on numbering.)*

## 9. Distribution

- **crates.io** — `cargo install vigia`. A name **cannot be reserved; it must be published**, and a publish is **permanent** (`cargo yank` hides a version from new dependents, it does not delete it, and the name stays taken). So the first publish ought to be a crate that does the minimal real thing. Verified free 2026-07-30, **and again 2026-08-08 against `index.crates.io` rather than the web API**. That index is the file `cargo` itself resolves against, so it is the only answer that binds. `vigia-core` is free too. That is not a courtesy check: see the publish-order bullet below.
- **Homebrew** — `homebrew-core` **cannot be reserved** and requires notability: ≥30 forks **or** ≥30 watchers **or** ≥75 stars, plus a stable versioned release. Until then a **personal tap** (`breferrari/homebrew-tap`) gives `brew install breferrari/tap/vigia` immediately, is fully under our control, and is what `cargo-dist` generates. Tap repository created 2026-08-08.
- **GitHub releases** — prebuilt binaries per platform via `cargo-dist`.
- No domain. Not needed for a CLI.
- **Two names are claimed, not one, and the order between them is forced.** `crates/vigia/Cargo.toml` declares `vigia-core` by `path` *and* `version`, and a registry will not accept a package whose dependency it cannot resolve, so `cargo publish -p vigia` fails outright with `no matching package named vigia-core found` until `vigia-core` exists on crates.io. The §6 crate split therefore spends a second permanent name. Nobody priced that cost at the time of the split, and it is better to state it than to discover it at the release. **The pipeline runs `cargo publish --workspace`**, one command rather than two. Cargo resolves the dependency order and waits for the index itself. The two-call form needs a sleep between the calls, and nobody knows the right length for it.
- **The release is one dispatch from the Actions tab, and every irreversible thing hangs off it.** `cargo-dist` 0.32 (the binary is named `dist`) builds the four target artifacts, creates the GitHub release, publishes the Homebrew formula to the tap, and runs a custom publish job that sends both crates to crates.io. `dist` has no built-in crates.io publisher: `publish-jobs` accepts `homebrew`, `npm` and custom reusable workflows, and the registry job is ours, in `.github/workflows/publish-crates-io.yml`. **That is what makes the tag the gate**: `RELEASE-SMOKE.md` runs against `dist build` output *before* the tag, not against a published artifact after it.

**Started by `bump.yml`, not by a tag push, and that is a mechanism rather than a preference.** `[workspace.metadata.dist] dispatch-releases = true` makes `release.yml` run on `workflow_dispatch` and **removes its tag-push trigger entirely**, so `git tag v0.1.2 && git push --tags` no longer produces a release. The reason is that the alternative needs a second permanent secret. A tag pushed by a workflow that holds `GITHUB_TOKEN` triggers no further workflow, because GitHub suppresses that to prevent recursion. `workflow_dispatch` is one of the two documented exceptions. So a bump job can start the release with the token it already has. Pushing a tag instead would need a PAT with write access to this repository. `bump.yml` takes *patch*, *minor* or *major* from a dropdown and works out the next version. It moves **both** version strings. It proves that the manifests still agree and that the built binary reports the new number. Only then does it commit and dispatch. Its `rehearse` option runs all of that and dispatches the tag `dry-run`. That is dist's own word for build everything and publish nothing, so the whole path can be exercised without spending a version. `crates/vigia/tests/package.rs::the_release_button_reaches_the_release` holds the three files together, because when they drift the result is a release that silently never happens after the version already moved.

**Starting the release needs no PAT. Committing the bump does, and the two were conflated until 2026-08-11.** The paragraph above is about the *trigger*, and it says nothing about the write. `main` is protected by seven required status checks. A commit pushed with `GITHUB_TOKEN` triggers no workflow, by the same suppression rule, so those checks can never arrive on it. The push is rejected with "7 of 7 required status checks are expected" however long anyone waits. The bot holds write rather than admin, so it cannot bypass the checks either. That is run 31435812487, the button's first real run. It cost nothing, because the ordering above verifies the version before committing it: `main` stayed at 0.1.0, no tag existed, nothing was published. So the commit is pushed with a fine-grained token whose owner is an admin of this repository, and the pre-flight checks two things before the version moves. **One token fills two roles**, and it is the `HOMEBREW_TAP_TOKEN` secret: it carries Contents read and write on the tap and on this repository, so it publishes the formula and moves the version. `bump.yml` reads it into a variable named for the second role, since that is what the step reasons about. `RELEASE-SMOKE.md` §0 records the sharing along with how to separate the two whenever that is wanted. They are different claims and neither implies the other. A real write to this repository, created and then deleted, covers the token's own grant. `.permissions.admin` covers the standing of the account behind it, since bypassing a protected branch is a property of a role rather than of a token. The second is a rule check rather than a probe, and the workflow labels it as one, because the only true probe of moving a protected branch is moving it, which is the irreversible act the whole step runs before.

**What the pre-flight deliberately does not check is whether `main` still lets admins bypass at all.** Reading branch protection needs admin rights on the API. `administration` is not a scope that a workflow can request under `permissions:`, so the job's own token cannot be given it. Giving it to the release token would widen a long-lived secret past `Contents` to buy a better error message. A better message is all it would buy. The step exists to prevent an irreversible half-step, a version moved or a crate published against a token that cannot finish. A protection setting that refuses the push is not one. The push is the last thing before the release is dispatched, so it fails with `main` unmoved, no tag and nothing published, which is exactly what run 31435812487 did. **And one premise stays unproven until a real push lands**, namely that a fine-grained token inherits its owner's bypass. `RELEASE-SMOKE.md` §0 records it as unproven, so that the guard does not imply otherwise. **Naming the token in the push URL is necessary and was not sufficient.** `actions/checkout` persists the workflow's own token into `.git/config` as an `Authorization` header, and git prefers a configured header over any userinfo in the remote URL. So the push went out as the bot whatever the URL said: every check above passed and the push was rejected exactly as before. The checkout therefore runs with `persist-credentials: false`, and that is the load-bearing half of the mechanism rather than a hardening detail. `crates/vigia/tests/package.rs::the_push_that_moves_main_is_authorised_before_the_version_does` is the gate that holds the shape. It asserts three things that each looked sufficient alone: that the checkout persists no credentials, that the remote the push names carries the token, and that the refspec is `HEAD` at the default branch.

**The registry publish runs last, and it is not atomic with the rest.** dist places every custom publish job `needs: [plan, host]`, and `host` is where `gh release create` runs, with no `--draft`. So the GitHub release and its binaries are public before the registry is touched. `announce` afterwards is a checkout. **The order is right for the reverse reason**: crates.io is the only step that cannot be undone, but a GitHub release can be deleted, a tap commit reverted and a tag moved. So the permanent action goes after every reversible step has already worked. A failure there leaves public binaries and an unclaimed name. Re-running the job recovers it. §5 of the checklist verifies the registry directly rather than inferring it from a green workflow.
- **Four targets in v0.1, and Linux is musl alone.** `x86_64-unknown-linux-musl`, `x86_64-apple-darwin`, `aarch64-apple-darwin`, `x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`. One static Linux binary that runs on any distribution is better than two that need selection logic, and `CLAUDE.md` already names musl as this project's static-binary story. **`aarch64-unknown-linux-musl` and `aarch64-pc-windows-msvc` are deferred**, not rejected. Both need a cross linker on the release runner, which is the component most likely to fail first on a release that nobody cut before. Neither is reachable by a user who cannot already use one of the four.
- **A published `.crate` does not carry `.github/`, and thirty of `vigia`'s test files read outside the package.** Counted by **file**, which is the unit that gets excluded; a read is not. Twenty-seven reach `vigia-core/tests/support/mod.rs` by `#[path = "../../…"]`: `budgets.rs`, `config.rs`, `first_paint.rs`, `follow.rs`, `hidden.rs`, `list.rs`, `mcp.rs`, `menu.rs`, `notes.rs`, `observe.rs`, `overview.rs`, `paint.rs`, `rail.rs`, `reads.rs`, `rows.rs`, `scroll.rs`, `select.rs`, `sheet.rs`, `single.rs`, `soak.rs`, `standing.rs`, `unreadable.rs`, `viewport.rs`, `wrap.rs`, `writes.rs`, `send.rs`, `socket.rs`. Five read repository documents through `CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR`, and **two of those five are in both groups**, keeping the total thirty rather than thirty-two: `soak.rs` reads `../../.github/workflows/soak.yml` and `sheet.rs` reads `README.md` to hold its gesture table against the sheet's ([#288](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/288)), and both also reach the fixture module; `package.rs`, which reads this file and three workflows, `theme_docs.rs`, which reads `docs/THEME.md`, and `register.rs`, which reads every `.rs` file under `crates/` to hold the comment ratchets, are in this group alone. `register.rs` climbs **one** level to reach a sibling crate where every other escape climbs two, so a scanner matching only the two-level shape misses it. All thirty are deliberate and none is a reason to weaken a gate. **The count and the names are gated rather than trusted**, by `crates/vigia/tests/package.rs::the_spec_names_every_test_that_escapes_the_package`, because prose held this number alone once and it drifted. **The fix, applied 2026-08-08 by [#12](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/12), is `exclude = ["tests/**"]` in `crates/vigia/Cargo.toml`**: the package ships no test suite rather than shipping one that cannot compile. `vigia-core` is not excluded and does not need to be, because its `tests/support/mod.rs` is inside its own package and `benches/engine.rs` reaches it without leaving.
- **A Windows upgrade displaces the old binary, and `vigia` removes it.** The documented upgrade renames `vigia.exe` to a fixed `vigia.exe.old`, so it recycles one file rather than adding one. The pane and the server each unlink that sibling of `current_exe()` at startup. **A held one refuses and is left for the next start.** Ruled 2026-09-09, reader.

- **An `mcs` tech pack ships from this repository too, and it distributes configuration rather than the binary.** `techpack.yaml` at the root, with `.techpack/` beside it, packages what the README teaches by hand for a reader who already keeps their Claude Code setup with [`mcs`](https://github.com/mcs-cli/mcs): the `vigia mcp` server at user scope, and the three hooks. **It is not a fourth way to get `vigia`**, so nothing above moves: the binary still arrives through the tap or crates.io, and the pack installs it by calling `brew`. It is third-party and optional in the sense that costs something. No part of the binary, the build, the release path or CI depends on it. The crate packages from `crates/vigia/`, so a published `.crate` never carries it. A reader who does not run `mcs` meets one line in the README. *Ruled 2026-09-10, reader.*

## 10. Open questions


- [x] ~~Does `gix` cover working-tree-vs-index diff at the fidelity and speed needed?~~ **Answered 2026-07-30: yes.** Hunk boundaries match `git diff -U3` exactly, with git as the oracle at every edit gap from 0 to 10 lines. On a 100k-line diff, release: first change available in 3.84ms against I4's 100ms, single-file re-diff 3.27ms p99 against I9's 16ms. The dependency with the power to force a rethink did not force one. Two corrections travel with the tick and neither undoes it. **The fidelity sweep ran over regular files only** ([#15](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/15)): a sweep along *content* cannot reach a mode that git stores by a different rule, and a symlink is a `120000` blob holding the target **path** while `vigia` was reading through the link. That was `std::fs::read` on this side of `gix`, so the answer holds. What it did not establish is coverage. §7's fixture-axis rule is where that lives now. **And the first-paint figure quoted here was the core's frame path, never the shipped one** ([#51](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/51)): `timings.rs` builds no `Highlighter`, so it measures open-plus-diff-one-file. Through the shell the first paint was **105.03ms** against I7's 50ms.


- [x] ~~Re-diffing every changed file costs 18.58ms p99 on a 100k-line diff, over the I9 budget.~~ **Closed 2026-07-30: I2a is enforced.** The frame path revalidates from the index blob, the change kind and a `stat`, so a single-line edit in a 100-file worktree recomputes exactly one diff and reads exactly that file. Over the same fixture, release: a real frame under continuous edits is **6.97ms p99**, revalidating 99 files and recomputing the one that moved. Pure revalidation with nothing edited is 3.93ms and reads **0 bytes**. A cold frame with nothing to reuse is 18.28ms and reads 3.6 MiB, which is the cost I2a removes.


- [x] ~~The settle margin is a fixed 2 seconds and can be narrowed per worktree from the modification times status already reports.~~ **Closed 2026-07-30: the margin stays at 2 seconds, and both halves of that were wrong.** ([#32](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/32).) **The estimator is unsound**, because it bounds the *smallest* granule observed while soundness needs the *largest* that occurs, and granularity is not uniform within one volume. Measured on the reference NTFS volume: 10,324 same-length rewrites over 3s produced 1,959 distinct stamps whose positive gaps spanned **502µs to 17,522µs, a 34.8x spread**, and a bulk write of 100 files produced a smallest cross-path gap of **998µs**. Narrowing the margin to 998µs would leave a real 17.5ms granule uncovered and would serve exactly the stale diff that `settled` prevents. Nothing passive does better: a monitor never writes, so it sees only the gaps that its user's tools left by chance, and those bound the granule from the wrong side. **And the breach was the fixture rather than the product.** Over the same event and 2.5s of frames, the core ran **98 of 182 frames over budget** and the shell ran **0 of 1060**, because I4 already makes the shell diff only what it draws. What is left is the core's own number, which is [#19](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/19)'s job.


- [x] **I3's day-long claim is met.** ([#47](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/47).) Reference machine, release, 100 x 500, **86,400 seconds**: 288 samples, **1,850,935 frames**, 1.15 TiB read, 36.3M lines highlighted, peak 36.32 MiB, 4 failed frames of 1.85M. Quarters **30.95 → 34.73 → 35.02 → 35.06 MiB**, with one step between the first pair and **+0.33 MiB across the entire back half**. The drift decelerates to ~0.02 MiB/h, against the **+81.3 MiB/h** that a deliberate 1 KiB-per-frame leak calibrates at. The 288-sample series is a fixture in `crates/vigia/tests/soak.rs`, so anyone can check this without running another day.


**The gate reads 13.26% and the process is flat, which is an instrument artifact rather than a breach.** The 10% warmup fraction puts the baseline on the ramp. From the measured plateau at sample 97 of 288, eight hours in, the same statistic reads **1.85% against the 5% budget**. [#126](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/126) ruled that the report **states** the measured plateau and its own drift beside the verdict, that the fraction stays the gate, and that the 5% budget is untouched. §7 carries the rule and the three detector designs that were tried and rejected. Where the warmup ends is a property of the fixture, not of the code: 20 x 200 plateaus in about thirty seconds and 100 x 500 takes about eight hours.


**A short window cannot call a residual slope, which is why the day was needed.** Two 900-second runs of the same code produced **−0.70 and +1.94 MiB/h**, and two 600-second runs produced **+7.89 and +2.74**. That is a 2.9x spread inside one window length, at both signs. **First scheduled run, 900 seconds, 40 x 500, all three tier-1 targets, green:** Linux 17,804 frames at 2.56% drift, macOS 6,053 at 2.49%, Windows 20,427 at 0.00%. So the observed spread of a **healthy** process is roughly **0 to 2.6%** against a 5% budget. That is real headroom, but not a lot of it: a longer window can cross the line on variation rather than on a leak. If it does, the answer is a measured warmup or a measured budget, never a wider one.


Two narrower gaps travel with this one. File handles can be counted only from `/proc`, so the descriptor gate has never run on the reference machine. CI on Linux exercises it for the first time, and it reports **9 descriptors at the first sample and 9 at the most**. And the measured window deliberately contains no index writes, because the loop reaches mass eviction by reverting files. So nothing soaks staging over a long run.


**The changed set is nothing like the fixtures, and that is the headline for the shelf.** The measurement ran on the reference machine, on this repository (93 tracked files, a 5.0 GiB `target/`), with an agent building and writing to it. It used four windows plus a `git status --porcelain` sampler at 1s: **3,960 samples across 4,942 seconds**. The maximum was **3** changed files and the mean was 0.85. There was a **clean tree 41.3%** of the time, and at most **one** file inside the settle margin at any sample. Every deferred cost on the shelf is a function of that count, and the fixtures that they were argued against are 100 and 2000 and ten thousand. What it does **not** license is a narrower budget: this is one agent on one small repository, and a `git reset`, a rebase or a formatter sweep are each one command away from four figures. It is evidence about likelihood, which is what [#19](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/19), [#16](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/16), [#48](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/48) and [#73](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/73) were deferred on.


**I1 is doing the most visible work in the report, and no fixture would have shown it.** The watch took **91,449 wakeups and filtered 91,252 of them, 99.8%, to produce 55 ticks**. That is a 5.0 GiB `target/` under a running build, and a synthetic worktree has no such thing. For the first **530 seconds** there were **zero frames** while tens of thousands of events arrived and were discarded.


**What breached is not what anyone was watching for.** One frame took **591.74ms** against I9's 16ms. It came fifty-four frames into a settled session, on one changed file and nine drawn rows, with **no key pressed**: the tick wrote the first Markdown that this process drew. The probe ran on a scratch tree rather than being assumed: first `.md` write **575.03ms**, second write to the same file **0.77ms**, first write to a *different* `.md` **4.70ms**. This is [#129](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/129). It is a Phase 8 row rather than a shelf entry because what is left is a hitch a reader feels.


**RSS is set by which grammars have compiled, not by the diff.** Four starting levels settle it: a window that started on a **clean** tree began at **8.96 MiB**, one with a single dirty `.rs` at **23.08**, one with `.rs` and Markdown both dirty at **44.99**. The soak's plateau over its own 20 x 200 fixture is 18.5 to 22.0 MiB, so a real repository sits **above** a fixture five times its size. The term that separates them is not the diff.


**The `git diff` oracle came back clean, on the checkout that most deserved it.** Thirty-four sweeps ran, each in its own process against its own `Worktree`. Each compared per-file `+N -M` against `git diff --numstat -z` in **both directions**, and the changed path set against `git status --porcelain -z`. Zero disagreements. The tree is `core.autocrlf=true` with LF blobs and CRLF on disk, which is [#65](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/65)'s exact configuration, and no fixture had that configuration until §7's fixture-axis rule was written.


**The harness left `Shell::draw`'s repaint out, so its first frame was the plain half of a frame the reader sees painted twice.** Everything at frame three or later is unaffected, which is where all four windows' headline figures sit. **Withdrawn**: a smoke run's *"max 6.48ms, none at or over budget"* over 13 frames. That maximum was the first frame, and the corrected harness measures the same shape at **558.83ms**.


> [!NOTE]

> **What is *not* open here, because it was a breach and is fixed**

>

> A hunk whose content changed used to throw its whole parse away. So the walk above was paid on *every* frame for as long as the file being read was the file being written: 520 lines per frame for a 22-row body, **53ms p99** four screenfuls in, sustained and with no input. A changed hunk now rewinds to the deepest parse position that the new content still agrees with, so a frame costs a screenful plus one `CHECKPOINT_STRIDE` at any depth: 40 lines and **11.47ms p99** on the same fixture, against 10.02ms at the top. Two lessons went into §7 rather than staying here: a budget measured at one position is measured at its cheapest one, and the caller-side gate that §7 asks for can be the *absolute* tier rather than only the structural one.


- [ ] Rename tracking cannot stream, so with it on the first change arrives at 97% of the walk and time-to-first equals time-to-last. Tracked by [#48](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/48). It is on by default anyway, because reporting a move as an unrelated delete plus add misdescribes what the agent did. Confirm against a week of real use, or paint without renames and reconcile them on a later frame. **One session has run and the question is nowhere near being asked**: the changed set peaked at **3** files over 3,960 samples, so a real session does not reach a walk whose cost is a concern at ten thousand. That is evidence about likelihood rather than about the walk, which is what this bullet is deferred on.


- [ ] The frame path walks status to completion before it reports a file list, so it does not stream the way the raw change iterator does. Tracked by [#48](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/48). **Two walks since [#313](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/313)** when the staged run is drawn. The second cannot stream at all: `gix`'s tree-index status is callback-driven, so the iterator over it is an iterator over a `Vec` that is already filled. It reads no content and measures 430µs against the other walk's 2.15ms on a 200-file fixture, so it changes the size of this question rather than its shape. Two reasons it costs nothing today: rename tracking cannot stream either and is on by default, and a scrollbar needs the file count regardless of how few files are drawn. What is open is whether both hold at ten thousand changed files, where the walk itself can exceed I4. Revisit with rename tracking, since they stand or fall together.


- [ ] An attributes change is a fourth way a cached artefact goes stale, and two of the three ways it can arrive are still invisible. Tracked by [#111](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/111). Writing a `.gitattributes` changes what git's clean filter does to files it does not touch, so a `FileDiff` or a `FileSpan` computed under the old rules describes bytes that are no longer the ones that a diff compares, while every term in the reuse rule reports "unchanged". `Frame::advance` drops both caches when a `.gitattributes` is in the changed set, which is what an agent writing one looks like. What that does **not** see is a change to `core.autocrlf` (configuration, not a file the status walk reports) or to `.git/info/attributes` (inside `.git`, which the walk does not enumerate). Both leave a stale diff on screen until something else touches the file. Neither has a gate, because reaching them from a test means changing configuration mid-session rather than writing a file. Measured before the guard existed: a file committed LF and held CRLF reported **80 rows where a cold frame computes 8**, indefinitely. `crates/vigia-core/tests/normalise.rs::a_running_frame_drops_what_it_cached_when_attributes_change` covers the file-shaped case on both caches, and mutation confirms it: clearing neither map, only the spans, or only the diffs each turns it red.


- [ ] The height walk is incremental now, and what remains is a syscall count rather than a read count. Tracked by [#19](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/19). [#101](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/101) took a tick over a hundred undrawn changed files from 16.98ms p50 to 9.40ms by replacing a read per file with a stat per file. The term that is left grows the same way: **500 changed files is 501 stats a tick, 15.01ms p50 and 16.82ms p99**. That is *over* I9 rather than at it, no gate runs at that scale, and 2000 is 26.06ms of stats before anything is drawn. #19 was filed against the *diff* path's revalidation, and the fix landed on a different one. The wall is the same wall. It has moved from a hundred files to about a thousand, and it is a property of how many `stat` calls a tick can afford rather than of either caller. The way out is not a faster stat. It is taking the fingerprints from the walk that `advance()` already performs, which means changing what `gix`'s status hands back.


- [ ] Highlighting a hunk is forward-only, so the first frame that draws deep inside a large hunk pays for everything above it there: landing on the last row of a 1006-line hunk costs the whole **60.97ms** parse, once. Tracked by [#51](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/51). `G`, a follow jump into a large file and scrolling **up** into the bottom of the previous file all land there directly. Scrolling down never does, because a screenful at a time is a screenful of parsing. §7 puts the cold path outside I9 by definition, so this is a first-touch cost rather than a breach, but it is a real hitch on one keypress. The fix, if daily use asks, is to bound the parse per frame and leave the tail unclassified until the next frame.


**A bounded parse is schedulable, and the reason once recorded here for why it was not is corrected rather than deleted, because it was cited to refuse things.** It said the redraw was one the event loop had no reason to schedule and that I1 forbade inventing a timer to get one. There is no timer to invent. [#262](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/262) added `Wake::Warmed`, a wake that the frame path sends itself off a background compile, and #51 closed by having the shell draw a second frame immediately (`App::owes_repaint`). Both are scheduled on **work** rather than on a clock, so I1's *0 wakeups while idle* does not reach either. **But neither is the mechanism a bounded parse would need.** `owes_repaint` is drained by an `if` and capped at exactly one extra frame, structurally. Written as a `while`, it spun at 100% CPU with the alternate screen held, because `paint` swallows a failed collect and the debt never clears. A parse bounded across N frames still needs someone to build a mechanism. What is open is whether daily use asks for the bound. What is settled is that I1 is not the reason it cannot be had.


**Daily use did ask, and the half that was fixable is fixed.** [#45](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/45) reported a fast scroll dropping frames. A *first* entry from below still costs the whole walk and is still the cold path: **25.01ms** for a 120-row hunk of wide-character Markdown against 139µs for a frame that reused it. What was not the cold path is a hunk that was re-entered after it left the screen. It paid 26.39ms for a parse that was in memory one frame earlier. `RETAINED_HUNKS` keeps four after they scroll off, so reversing direction over ground already read costs **397µs**. A constant added to the viewport bounds this, not the session, so I3 sees a slightly higher plateau rather than drift. **And the per-line cost is a function of line length, not of the script**: 660-byte Markdown parses at **0.32µs a byte** against **1.76µs a byte** for 34-byte Rust lines, so wide content is *cheaper* per byte and expensive only because the lines are nineteen times longer. The attribution ran the identical bytes under an extension that `syntect` has no grammar for: **708µs of a 764µs collect** at p99 is the parse.


- [ ] The heat projection is the one drawn thing whose cost follows the file rather than the window. Tracked by [#55](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/55). Placing a change needs its working-tree line number, and a line number is known only by counting the lines before it, so `heat_of` walks every line of a drawn file's hunks even though the viewport shows a screenful. Everything else in the shell follows the screen. This is not a read, so `reads.rs` does not move and I2a is untouched. It is arithmetic over lines already in memory, and it runs only for rows actually drawn.


**What "actually drawn" means has grown twice with the pane it is measured on**, which is what this bullet has to be re-measured against when it is next picked up. The bound is the pinned list's **drawn height**, plus the headings in the diff, plus the file the viewport is sitting inside. That was a flat six rows, then a share of the pane ([#160](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/160)), which is twelve on a fifty-row pane, and **beside a rail there is no cap at all** ([#252](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/252)), so a fifty-row pane draws forty-six rows. What the rail did not leave unmeasured is the *frame*: a 200x40 pane that draws thirty-six pinned rows takes **0.71ms p50 and 0.79ms p99 against I9's 16ms**. That is the worst of three rounds interleaved with the 80x24 screen that every other budget is taken on, which read 0.99ms and 1.36ms. So the worst case grew and the frame it sits in did not. This bullet records it rather than assuming it away, because the shape is the one I4 exists to prevent even where this instance is affordable. If it ever matters, the fix is to carry the line number on `Line` rather than recomputing it, which is the same information the gutter already derives twice.


- [ ] Default view: unstaged only, or working-tree-vs-HEAD? Unstaged is the thesis. Confirm against a week of real use. Tracked by [#50](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/50). **Narrowed 2026-08-25 by [#313](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/313)**: a reader can now draw both comparisons with `a`, so this stopped being *which comparison the tool is about* and became *which way the toggle starts*. That is a view default, and therefore [#306](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/306)'s file. The week of use still decides it, and the cost of getting it wrong is now one keystroke rather than a redefinition of every drawn element.


- [ ] Windows: supported target or best-effort? This is a posture question with no work left in it: whether the README and the release notes say *supported* or *best-effort*. It belongs with the first release. `ROADMAP.md` has recorded that placement since before the technical halves closed. Tracked by [#11](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/11).


**The colour half closed 2026-07-31**: degradation is a mechanism with a gate over every rung rather than a hope, so a console that cannot draw 24-bit gets 256 by detection and not by accident.


**The glyph half closed 2026-08-17 by [#159](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/159), and what made it decidable is that this bullet was wrong about its own mechanism.** *"There is no equivalent for characters"* is true of **probing** and false of **deciding**: no terminal reports which glyphs its font carries, and `Depth` never asked one either. It reads `TERM_PROGRAM` and `TERM` and consults a table of what somebody checked. `Glyphs` picks `block`, `braille` or `octant` the same way. This was measured rather than assumed: **Consolas, which the legacy console draws with, carries none of U+2800**, while Cascadia Mono and Cascadia Code carry all of it, so Windows without `WT_SESSION` takes blocks and Windows Terminal takes braille. That is the exact inverse of `Depth`'s Windows rung, and the asymmetry is the finding rather than an inconsistency: **colour aged out on that console and glyphs did not, because the font did not age with it.** Octants are reachable only by `VIGIA_GLYPHS=octant` until [#324](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/324) teaches `Glyphs::detect` the 2026 matrix, which pins the four engines that self-draw the range natively (ghostty 1.2+, foot 1.20+, kitty 0.40+ and VTE 0.78+). That is the same way this bullet already argued for braille.


**The honest residue is everything outside CP437 that is not the sparkline.** Outside: the caret `▸`, the follow marker `▶`, the pulse mark `●`, the elision `…`, the continuation `›`, the downward continuation `↳`, the rename arrow `←`, the scrollbar's step buttons `▲` and `▼`, the resolved mark `✓`, the `✕` the sheet's close control and a note's left side share, and B21's note icon `✎` and note bar `▎`, for which `»` and `▌` are recorded as the CP437 stand-ins, with `└` for `↳`, for the day when a ladder for this residue exists. **The rounded corners `╭╮╰╯` are outside it too and are the one entry with a ladder already**: the sheet, the box and a committed note's enclosure take the square corners on `Glyphs::Block`. Inside: `█`, `▄`, the heat strip's `■` at 0xFE, the header separator `·`, the rule `─`, the sparkline track `_`, the scrollbar track `│`, and the box's sides, which are those two. **Nothing detects or reports it, and that is why this stays open rather than closing: a rule that happens to be free once is not a ladder.**

## 11. Behaviour

§3 says how well `vigia` does things. Every number there is defensible and has a test. §3 does not say **what happens when you press a key, or when nothing has changed**. Without that, the product reads as a screenshot with budgets attached.

This section has two parts. §11.1 records what the shell already does, because that was decided in code first. §11.2 records what is still undecided.

> [!WARNING]
> **Behaviour decided in code without a line here is a defect**
>
> The README says this file is the source of truth, written before the code. On 2026-07-30 that was **not true** for the whole interaction surface: the keymap and the treatment of untracked files were both settled in the code and appeared nowhere above. §11.1 fixes that. The rule it leaves has §3's shape. An invariant without a failing test does not count, and **a behaviour without a spec line is an accident.** The pre-flight of `take-next` catches neither, because it compares invariant tokens, not behaviour.

### 11.1 What the shell does today

Where this disagrees with the code, the code is the bug.

**What the diff contains.** By default the diff is the working tree against the index. It shows what the agent in the other pane just wrote, and it is the smallest useful thing. `a` adds the index against `HEAD` beside it as a second run (§11.2 B17). Which way that toggle *starts* is still open, as [#50](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/50). Untracked files are included and diff as all-additions. Agents create files often, and a tool blind to new files would miss its own use case. Rename tracking is on by default, because a move shown as an unrelated delete plus add misdescribes what happened. Its streaming cost is open in §10. *Ruled 2026-07-30, session.*

**`b` and `B` move where the pane stands, which is a separate question from what it contains.** The paragraph above says what the diff holds. This one says what it is measured *from*. The default is `current`, the live pane: the working tree against the index. `b` stands the pane at the **branch point**. The run then holds everything since that point, each path once: what this branch committed, what is staged, and what is written but not staged. These are composed so that a file added in a commit and deleted in the working tree reads as removed, not as added. `b` is a toggle, not a mode. No key changes meaning while it is on, and `a` is unchanged. The staged run still means the index against `HEAD` and is still drawn beside. Under `since`, that run is part of what the pane already holds, and it is exactly what a reader who presses `a` asked to see. *Ruled 2026-09-10, reader* ([#490](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/490)).

**`B` opens the list of every place the pane can stand, and so does a click on the token.** The token is the pointer's own target. Whatever the token says, a left press on its cells opens the list. At rest, a `▾` after the word shows this, which a hover mark cannot do. `b` keeps its toggle, the way every setting kept its letter when the config menu arrived. The branch point stays one key away, and the list reaches every other place. The box is B22's overlay over rows already drawn, so nothing moves when it opens. It closes the way the gestures sheet does: `Esc`, its own control, or a press outside. **Its title is the reading**, so the box says what choosing a row will do without spending a row on it. Under `since`, its top two rows are `current` and the branch point. The branch point is named by the branch, not by an id. Every row below them is a commit with its abbreviation, its age and its subject. The caret opens on the row the pane stands on. **The walk pages.** The revwalk of `gix` is an iterator, so the box costs one screenful of history and extends by another when the caret comes within a row of the end. Each page carries the count of commits it stepped over, so a gate can watch it stop. Buffering the whole history would cost I4, and nothing else here would. *`B` and the chevron ruled 2026-09-11, reader; the rest the same day, session* ([#508](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/508)).

**The token carries one of three words, and `O` reads a commit the other way.** `current` is the special case of `since` whose point is the index. `only <id>` is the other reading of the same grammar: that commit alone. It compares its first parent's tree against its own, and a root commit against the empty tree, as `git show` does. The two words suggest each other, so a reader who meets one guesses the other exists. **`O` flips the reading**, with the list closed or open. It does not move the pane. It changes only what the diff is measured *between*. Where the position names no commit, the pane stays and the footer says why. This is `b`'s rule one step further out. `current` has no commit at all. The branch point is a commit the *other* branch made, so reading it alone would draw work this branch did not do. Under `only`, the list drops those two rows, and their absence is the explanation. *Ruled 2026-09-11, session* ([#509](https://github.com/breferrari/vigia/issues/509)).

**`since` is live and `only` is a still picture. That difference is the whole cost of the second reading.** Under `since`, the working tree is one of the ends, so nothing below applies. Under `only`, both ends are trees in the object database. Five things on screen would then describe something other than what is shown, so each is switched off at one lever, read from the frame the rows came from. **Notes**: B21 carries a note to the agent beside the pane, and a historical line gives the agent nothing to act on. So there is no `✎`, no note row and no count, and neither `c` nor a press on the gutter does anything. **`a`**: the staged run is the index against `HEAD`, and it exists only where the comparison includes the index. It is neither walked nor counted, and the key does nothing. **`f`**: there is no newest change to follow, so the key does nothing and the indicator is not drawn. The flag is kept, so follow returns as it was when the reading switches back. **The sparkline and the pulse**, and the recency ramp beside them, are the watch's time series over the last `HISTORY_WINDOW`. They describe now while the body shows the past, so the rows draw the *untracked* rung that §5.1 already defines. **The heat strip stays**, because it is positional within a file and can be computed from the commit's own diff. The store keeps filling throughout, so the reading is correct, not empty, when it switches back. *Ruled 2026-09-11, session.*

**While the pane is parked, a write to the tree shows on the footer and does nothing else.** I5 says correct with zero interaction. A pane standing at a past commit cannot show what just happened, so it must at least say that something happened. That is one line in the `Arrived` voice that unasked-for news already uses, arriving over `NOTICE_ARRIVING` and held for `ARRIVED_LINGER`. It counts what the burst wrote and names the working tree, so it cannot be read as the commit's own count. The tick does nothing else: no walk, no arrival marks, no follow. So I1 improves here rather than degrades, and the body stays still by construction. *Ruled 2026-09-11, session.*

**A named row shows the facts of the run it names. A commit row shows none.** The rule above applies inside the box too. The row the pane stands on reads its count and total from the view the header just drew, so the two cannot disagree about one run. The other named row is a run this frame is not drawing, so it is walked for. That walk uses **one frame kept for as long as the box is open**, not one made per wake, and that difference is the budget. A fresh frame costs the whole run: **2.53ms** over three files and **20.21ms** over two hundred. A kept frame costs the edit: **2.03ms** and **5.93ms**, against I9's 16ms. One page of history costs **0.85ms**. Only one frame is kept. So when the pane stands at a commit the list named, `current` keeps its facts and the branch point draws a name only. A commit is a point, not a run, so a commit row counts nothing. The total belongs on the row the pane lands on when that row is chosen. *Ruled 2026-09-11, session.*

**A path absent from both ends is not a row. A path whose content came back is a row.** A file the branch added and the working tree then deleted was never at the branch point and is not on disk. Nothing happened to it, and `git diff <base>` agrees. A `removed` row would claim the reader deleted something the branch point never had. A file edited and then edited back is a row with no hunks under it. Proving it unchanged would need a read per path, and I4 says a walk does not read content. The two cases look alike. They differ in what it costs to tell them apart. **A rename the branch made survives an edit on top of it**, because where a file came from is a fact about the base end. Nothing re-detects a rename *across* the two walks. A file removed in a commit and added in the working tree under another name reads as a delete and an add, because each walk alone sees only that. *Ruled 2026-09-10, session.*

**The branch point is a merge base. The branch it is measured against comes from a fixed order, not a setting.** First comes the branch's own upstream, because a reader who set one has already said what this branch came off. Then `main`, then `master`, each as a local branch and as `origin/`'s, because a repository that has never pushed and a repository that is only a clone are both ordinary. A branch that is its own upstream is skipped: its merge base would be `HEAD` itself, and an empty run that nothing on screen explains is worse than a refusal. When nothing in the order resolves, the key **refuses on the footer and the pane stays where it was**. This is B3's rule about saying what a comparison is empty for, applied one step earlier. The point is resolved once, on the first press, and kept. Resolving it every frame would put a revision walk on the frame path that I9 pays for, and a base that moved would change the diff while the tree did not. **The point is kept until a walk from it fails**, and resolved again on the next press. A branch can be rebased, amended or garbage-collected under a pane that has been open for days. A base the object database has lost would otherwise fail the same way for the life of the process. A walk that fails on the way *back* to the working tree says nothing about the base, so the base is kept. **If the base disappears while the pane stands at it, the pane returns to the live view.** The tick is the only walk that runs with nobody there to press anything. A pane left at a lost base would hold its last good picture for the life of the process, and on a monitor that looks the same as a tree that stopped changing. The live pane is the one comparison that is always available, so a lost base lands there, with the failure on the footer. A status walk that fails once is retried on the next wake and does not move the pane. *Ruled 2026-09-10, session.*
